### ScreenLLM: Stateful Screen Schema for Efficient Action Understanding and Prediction
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.20978v1)
- **Authors**: Yiqiao Jin, Stefano Petrangeli, Yu Shen, Gang Wu
- **Abstract**: Graphical User Interface (GUI) agents are autonomous systems that interpret and generate actions, enabling intelligent user assistance and automation. Effective training of these agent presents unique challenges, such as sparsity in supervision signals, scalability for large datasets, and the need for nuanced user understanding. We propose stateful screen schema, an efficient representation of GUI interactions that captures key user actions and intentions over time. Building on this foundation, we introduce ScreenLLM, a set of multimodal large language models (MLLMs) tailored for advanced UI understanding and action prediction. Extensive experiments on both open-source and proprietary models show that ScreenLLM accurately models user behavior and predicts actions. Our work lays the foundation for scalable, robust, and intelligent GUI agents that enhance user interaction in diverse software environments.
- **Summary**: Okay, I've reviewed the provided document. Here's a summary and a critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces ScreenLLM, a framework for understanding and predicting user actions in graphical user interfaces (GUIs). It addresses challenges like data sparsity, scalability, and nuanced user understanding by proposing a "stateful screen schema." This schema is a compact, informative textual representation of GUIs and user interactions, capturing actions and intentions over time. ScreenLLM then uses multimodal large language models (MLLMs), tailored for UI understanding, to predict actions based on the screen schema. The paper demonstrates the effectiveness of ScreenLLM through experiments on both open-source and proprietary models, showing improvements in user understanding and action prediction.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The main novelty lies in the *stateful screen schema* and its application to GUI understanding with MLLMs. Representing the dynamic state of a GUI using a schema, rather than relying solely on frame-by-frame analysis, seems to be a reasonable idea.The approach provides a textual history that is beneficial for LLMs and is useful since GUIs have sparse changes between frames. It's a clever approach to solve data sparsity and scalability by extracting keyframes. The idea of the model using the textual history summary to predict the next step makes intuitive sense.
*   **Significance:** The significance of the paper hinges on whether ScreenLLM provides a substantial improvement over existing methods. The improvements demonstrated in the experimental results(40.7% improvement in BLEU-2 and 16.5% in ROUGE-L) suggests potential. GUI automation and intelligent assistance are fields with significant practical applications, and a more efficient and accurate approach to understanding user behavior could have a real-world impact. The findings also have implications for improving the instruction-following capabilities of LLMs.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   The *stateful screen schema* is a well-motivated and potentially effective approach to address the challenges of GUI understanding.
    *   The empirical validation demonstrates improved performance over baselines on different tasks. The use of both open-source and proprietary models enhances the credibility of the results.
    *   The paper is well-structured and clearly explains the proposed framework.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   While the results show improvements, the specific magnitude of the improvements could be more deeply analysed. What types of examples does it work well on, and which does it struggle with?
    *   The descriptions of certain components, such as the cursor detection CNN and the memory module are relatively brief. More details would be needed for others to replicate the work.
    *   The evaluation primarily focuses on action understanding and prediction. It would be beneficial to see how ScreenLLM performs in real-world GUI automation tasks.
    *   Although the stateful screen schema is mentioned to solve for data scarcity and scalability, further analysis could be provided to better understand these benefits.
*   **Potential Influence:**
    *   The paper may inspire further research in GUI understanding and automation, with a focus on state representation and the use of MLLMs.
    *   The proposed framework could be used as a foundation for developing more intelligent user assistants and automation tools for various software applications.

**Score: 7**

**Rationale:**

The paper presents a novel and well-motivated approach to GUI understanding using stateful screen schema and MLLMs. It demonstrates improved performance compared to baselines, suggesting its potential for real-world applications. However, the paper could benefit from more detailed descriptions of certain components, a more in-depth analysis of the results, and an evaluation in real-world GUI automation scenarios. Overall, it is a solid contribution to the field, with potential for future research and practical applications.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Patients Speak, AI Listens: LLM-based Analysis of Online Reviews Uncovers Key Drivers for Urgent Care Satisfaction
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.20981v1)
- **Authors**: Xiaoran Xu, Zhaoqian Xue, Chi Zhang, Jhonatan Medri, Junjie Xiong, Jiayan Zhou, Jin Jin, Yongfeng Zhang, Siyuan Ma, Lingyao Li
- **Abstract**: Investigating the public experience of urgent care facilities is essential for promoting community healthcare development. Traditional survey methods often fall short due to limited scope, time, and spatial coverage. Crowdsourcing through online reviews or social media offers a valuable approach to gaining such insights. With recent advancements in large language models (LLMs), extracting nuanced perceptions from reviews has become feasible. This study collects Google Maps reviews across the DMV and Florida areas and conducts prompt engineering with the GPT model to analyze the aspect-based sentiment of urgent care. We first analyze the geospatial patterns of various aspects, including interpersonal factors, operational efficiency, technical quality, finances, and facilities. Next, we determine Census Block Group(CBG)-level characteristics underpinning differences in public perception, including population density, median income, GINI Index, rent-to-income ratio, household below poverty rate, no insurance rate, and unemployment rate. Our results show that interpersonal factors and operational efficiency emerge as the strongest determinants of patient satisfaction in urgent care, while technical quality, finances, and facilities show no significant independent effects when adjusted for in multivariate models. Among socioeconomic and demographic factors, only population density demonstrates a significant but modest association with patient ratings, while the remaining factors exhibit no significant correlations. Overall, this study highlights the potential of crowdsourcing to uncover the key factors that matter to residents and provide valuable insights for stakeholders to improve public satisfaction with urgent care.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Patients Speak, AI Listens: LLM-based Analysis of Online Reviews Uncovers Key Drivers for Urgent Care Satisfaction" investigates patient satisfaction in urgent care facilities using a crowdsourcing approach. The authors collect Google Maps reviews from the DMV area and Florida and use prompt engineering with GPT models to perform aspect-based sentiment analysis (ABSA). The paper analyzes geospatial patterns of different aspects of urgent care experiences (interpersonal factors, operational efficiency, technical quality, finances, and facilities).  It then links these sentiments to CBG-level socioeconomic and demographic characteristics to understand what factors influence patient perceptions. The study concludes that interpersonal factors and operational efficiency are the strongest determinants of patient satisfaction, while socioeconomic factors have limited impact.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The combination of crowdsourced data (Google Maps reviews), large language models (LLMs) for ABSA, and socioeconomic analysis to understand patient satisfaction in urgent care presents a level of novelty. While individual components of this approach have been used before in healthcare or related fields, integrating them in this specific context is a valuable contribution.
* **Significance:**  The identification of interpersonal factors and operational efficiency as primary drivers of patient satisfaction in urgent care is a practically significant finding. This allows healthcare providers and policymakers to focus on improving these areas, potentially leading to better patient outcomes and resource allocation.  The use of publicly available online reviews is a cost-effective and scalable approach to gather insights that would be difficult or expensive to obtain through traditional survey methods.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Comprehensive Approach:** The study uses a robust methodology by combining LLM-based ABSA with geospatial and statistical analysis.
    * **Practical Implications:** The findings offer actionable insights for urgent care facilities. The paper clearly identifies key areas for improvement.
    * **Scalability and Cost-Effectiveness:** Using Google Maps reviews and LLMs provides a scalable and less resource-intensive approach to gather patient feedback compared to traditional surveys.
    * **Clear and Well-Defined Research Questions:** The research questions are well-articulated and addressed systematically.
    * **Sensitivity analysis:** Addressing potential bias with varying analysis sample size

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Data Bias:** Google Maps reviewers may not be representative of the entire population.  Access to technology and willingness to leave reviews could skew the data.  This point should be discussed more thoroughly and acknowledged.
    * **LLM Accuracy:** Although the authors evaluated several LLMs, the potential for errors or biases in LLM-based sentiment analysis exists. The dataset is not perfectly labeled (a common challenge)
    * **Limited Socioeconomic Impact:** While the study found limited influence from socioeconomic factors, this may be due to the selection of CBG-level data or a focus on specific socioeconomic variables.  Other factors (e.g., access to transportation, health literacy) may also play a role.
    * **Causation vs. Correlation:** The study identifies associations, but it does not prove causation. It's possible that other unmeasured factors influence both sentiment and patient satisfaction.
    * **Generalizability:** The findings are specific to the DMV and Florida regions. Further research is needed to determine if the results are generalizable to other geographic areas.
    * **Sentiment Class Imbalance:** The sentiment classification exhibited a significant imbalance, with many more positive samples than negative or neutral samples.
* **Potential Influence:** The paper could influence healthcare researchers to explore other crowdsourcing and LLM-based methods for patient feedback analysis. It could also encourage urgent care facilities to actively monitor and respond to online reviews, focusing on interpersonal skills and operational efficiency.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a solid and well-executed study that integrates multiple methods to gain insights into patient satisfaction in urgent care. The use of LLMs for ABSA in this context is valuable and contributes to the field. The practical implications for urgent care facilities are clear and actionable.  However, the limitations related to data bias, LLM accuracy, and generalizability prevent it from being a truly exceptional work. The analysis and evaluation of limitations could be expanded.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### FinAudio: A Benchmark for Audio Large Language Models in Financial Applications
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.20990v1)
- **Authors**: Yupeng Cao, Haohang Li, Yangyang Yu, Shashidhar Reddy Javaji, Yueru He, Jimin Huang, Zining Zhu, Qianqian Xie, Xiao-yang Liu, Koduvayur Subbalakshmi, Meikang Qiu, Sophia Ananiadou, Jian-Yun Nie
- **Abstract**: Audio Large Language Models (AudioLLMs) have received widespread attention and have significantly improved performance on audio tasks such as conversation, audio understanding, and automatic speech recognition (ASR). Despite these advancements, there is an absence of a benchmark for assessing AudioLLMs in financial scenarios, where audio data, such as earnings conference calls and CEO speeches, are crucial resources for financial analysis and investment decisions. In this paper, we introduce \textsc{FinAudio}, the first benchmark designed to evaluate the capacity of AudioLLMs in the financial domain. We first define three tasks based on the unique characteristics of the financial domain: 1) ASR for short financial audio, 2) ASR for long financial audio, and 3) summarization of long financial audio. Then, we curate two short and two long audio datasets, respectively, and develop a novel dataset for financial audio summarization, comprising the \textsc{FinAudio} benchmark. Then, we evaluate seven prevalent AudioLLMs on \textsc{FinAudio}. Our evaluation reveals the limitations of existing AudioLLMs in the financial domain and offers insights for improving AudioLLMs. All datasets and codes will be released.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces FINAUDIO, a novel benchmark designed to evaluate Audio Large Language Models (AudioLLMs) in the financial domain.  FINAUDIO includes three tasks: ASR for short financial audio clips, ASR for long financial audio recordings, and summarization of long financial audio. The benchmark utilizes existing and newly curated datasets, amounting to over 400 hours of financial audio. The authors evaluate seven prominent AudioLLMs on FINAUDIO, revealing limitations of existing models in this domain. The paper highlights the potential of open-source models like Whisper-v3 and emphasizes the need for improvements in handling financial terminology and numerical information.

**Critical Evaluation:**

The paper addresses a significant gap in the field.  While there's increasing attention on AudioLLMs and financial NLP, the intersection of these areas is underexplored.  The lack of a dedicated benchmark makes it difficult to systematically assess and compare AudioLLMs in financial contexts.  The creation of FINAUDIO is a worthwhile contribution.

**Strengths:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary strength lies in its creation of FINAUDIO, the *first* AudioLLM benchmark specifically for the financial domain. This fills a clear void.
*   **Task Design:**  The chosen tasks are well-motivated by real-world financial applications, such as analyzing earnings calls and investor interactions. The tasks are ASR (short and long audio) and Financial audio summarization.
*   **Dataset Curation:** The effort involved in collecting and creating datasets is substantial, particularly the newly created FinAudioSum dataset.
*   **Systematic Evaluation:**  The evaluation of seven different AudioLLMs provides valuable insights into their strengths and weaknesses in the financial domain. The prompt robustness analysis is a nice touch.
*   **Practical Implications:** The finding that open-source models like Whisper-v3 perform well (and sometimes better than closed-source) has practical implications for cost-effectiveness and privacy-preserving solutions in finance.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Summarization Performance:** The summarization performance of the evaluated models is reported as moderate, which is somewhat underwhelming. This could indicate a limitation of the AudioLLMs, the datasets, the evaluation metrics, or all of the above. The paper might benefit from a more in-depth analysis of why summarization is challenging. The use of LLM APIs such as GPT-4 is also a limitation, and only tests summarization given perfect transcriptions.
*   **Error Analysis:** While an error analysis is performed, it is relatively brief and could be more detailed. Specific examples of errors could be useful.
*   **Limited Scope:** The limitations section acknowledges that the benchmark is focused on ASR-related tasks and English-language audio. This limits the generalizability of the findings.
*   **Lack of Fine-tuning:** A potential improvement would be to explore fine-tuning AudioLLMs on the financial audio data and then evaluating their performance. This could provide a better understanding of their potential and highlight the value of the FINAUDIO dataset. This would require significantly more work, but be a more substantial contribution.
*   **Baselines:** There is no comparison to other audio summarization tools.

**Significance:**

The paper's significance lies in establishing a framework for evaluating and improving AudioLLMs in the financial domain. This could spur further research in this area and ultimately lead to better tools for financial analysis, investment decision-making, and customer service. The public release of the datasets and code will encourage broader participation in this research area.

**Overall:**

FINAUDIO is a valuable contribution to the field of financial NLP and AudioLLMs. It addresses a clear gap and provides a solid foundation for future research. While there are areas for improvement, the novelty and practical implications of the work justify a reasonably high score.

**Score: 7**

**Rationale:**
A score of 7 reflects the paper's clear novelty and practical value in establishing a benchmark for AudioLLMs in the financial domain. The collection of datasets, systematic evaluation, and prompt robustness analysis are all valuable contributions. The limitations section acknowledges potential areas of future improvement, such as including other languages and exploring fine-tuning. While some of the results (especially summarization) are moderate and the error analysis could be more in-depth, the paper overall provides a valuable starting point and significantly advances the field.

- **Classification**: cs.CE
- **Score**: 7/10

### Multi-head Reward Aggregation Guided by Entropy
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.20995v1)
- **Authors**: Xiaomin Li, Xupeng Chen, Jingxuan Fan, Eric Hanchen Jiang, Mingye Gao
- **Abstract**: Aligning large language models (LLMs) with safety guidelines typically involves reinforcement learning from human feedback (RLHF), relying on human-generated preference annotations. However, assigning consistent overall quality ratings is challenging, prompting recent research to shift towards detailed evaluations based on multiple specific safety criteria. This paper uncovers a consistent observation: safety rules characterized by high rating entropy are generally less reliable in identifying responses preferred by humans. Leveraging this finding, we introduce ENCORE, a straightforward entropy-guided approach that composes multi-head rewards by downweighting rules exhibiting high rating entropy. Theoretically, we demonstrate that rules with elevated entropy naturally receive minimal weighting in the Bradley-Terry optimization framework, justifying our entropy-based penalization. Through extensive experiments on RewardBench safety tasks, our method significantly surpasses several competitive baselines, including random weighting, uniform weighting, single-head Bradley-Terry models, and LLM-based judging methods. Our proposed approach is training-free, broadly applicable to various datasets, and maintains interpretability, offering a practical and effective solution for multi-attribute reward modeling.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces ENCORE (ENtropy-penalized Compositional REwarding), a novel training-free method for aggregating multi-head rewards in large language model (LLM) safety alignment.  The core idea is based on the observation that safety rules with high rating entropy (indicating less reliable assessments) tend to correlate with lower accuracy in predicting human preferences. ENCORE addresses this by downweighting rules with high entropy when composing the final reward signal. This method avoids the need for additional training or hyperparameter tuning and maintains interpretability.  The paper provides theoretical justification rooted in the Bradley-Terry model, demonstrating that high-entropy rules naturally receive minimal weights during gradient-based weight optimization.  Experiments on the RewardBench safety benchmark show ENCORE outperforms several baselines, including uniform weighting, random weighting, single-head Bradley-Terry models, and LLM-based judging methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The key novelty lies in its recognition and exploitation of the relationship between rating entropy and rule accuracy in the context of multi-head reward modeling. While entropy is a well-established concept, its application as a *direct* penalty (through a re-weighting scheme) for multi-head rewards in LLM alignment is relatively novel.  The theoretical analysis using the Bradley-Terry model adds further depth to the understanding of this approach.

**Significance:** The significance of ENCORE stems from its simplicity, efficiency, and interpretability.  It offers a practical and effective solution for multi-attribute reward modeling *without* requiring additional training data or computationally expensive hyperparameter tuning. This is a significant advantage, especially considering the resource demands associated with training and aligning LLMs. The improved performance on RewardBench is also a strong indicator of its practical value. ENCORE helps to address the challenge of inconsistency when LLMs are directly assessing responses because different LLMs have distinct knowledge base and preference with respect to safety and truthfulness.

**Strengths:**

*   **Simplicity and Efficiency:**  ENCORE is computationally lightweight and training-free, making it easy to integrate into existing RLHF pipelines.
*   **Interpretability:**  The entropy-penalized weighting scheme is transparent, allowing for a clear understanding of the relative importance and reliability of different safety rules.
*   **Strong Empirical Results:**  ENCORE demonstrates significant performance improvements over competitive baselines on the RewardBench benchmark.
*   **Theoretical Justification:** The analysis based on the Bradley-Terry model provides a solid theoretical foundation for the approach.
*   **Generalizability:** The entropy-accuracy correlation appears to be robust across different datasets and model architectures, suggesting good generalization potential.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Reliance on Initial LLM Judgment Quality:** ENCORE relies on initial ratings from a multi-head reward model, so its performance is indirectly tied to the underlying quality of those initial judgments. If the initial LLM's judgment is flawed, ENCORE will be penalizing an already bad rule. While the approach is training-free (step 2), the model still needs to fine-tune the multi-head reward model (step 1).
*   **Limited Exploration of Alternative Entropy Measures:** The paper primarily focuses on discrete entropy. While the authors acknowledge the exploration of differential entropy in the appendix, it might be beneficial to discuss other entropy measures or variations in more detail.
*   **Limited Ablation Studies:** While the paper does include ablation experiments, further exploration of different temperature parameters (τ) and alternative penalization functions could provide additional insights.
*  **No Direct RLHF experiment:** Although the experiments clearly display the effectiveness of the proposed method in rewarding training models, it is unknown whether better reward signals translate to improvements in the RLHF fine-tuning.

**Potential Influence:** ENCORE has the potential to influence the field by providing a readily applicable and interpretable method for improving the alignment of LLMs with safety guidelines. Its simplicity and effectiveness could lead to its adoption in various RLHF pipelines. It could also inspire further research into leveraging other statistical properties of rule ratings to enhance reward modeling.

**Score:** 7.5

**Justification:**

The paper presents a novel and well-supported method that addresses a significant challenge in LLM safety alignment. Its simplicity, efficiency, and interpretability are major strengths. The empirical results are compelling, and the theoretical analysis provides a solid foundation.
However, the dependency on initial LLM ratings and somewhat limited exploration of different settings are the weaknesses of the paper. Additionally, the paper could have a greater impact if the authors consider including direct RLHF fine-tuning of the training model. Therefore, a score of 7.5 reflects the paper's substantial contribution while acknowledging some limitations.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Evaluating Large Language Models for Automated Clinical Abstraction in Pulmonary Embolism Registries: Performance Across Model Sizes, Versions, and Parameters
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.21004v1)
- **Authors**: Mahmoud Alwakeel, Emory Buck, Jonathan G. Martin, Imran Aslam, Sudarshan Rajagopal, Jian Pei, Mihai V. Podgoreanu, Christopher J. Lindsell, An-Kwok Ian Wong
- **Abstract**: Pulmonary embolism (PE) is a leading cause of cardiovascular mortality, yet our understanding of optimal management remains limited due to heterogeneous and inaccessible radiology documentation. The PERT Consortium registry standardizes PE management data but depends on resource-intensive manual abstraction. Large language models (LLMs) offer a scalable alternative for automating concept extraction from computed tomography PE (CTPE) reports. This study evaluated the accuracy of LLMs in extracting PE-related concepts compared to a human-curated criterion standard. We retrospectively analyzed MIMIC-IV and Duke Health CTPE reports using multiple LLaMA models. Larger models (70B) outperformed smaller ones (8B), achieving kappa values of 0.98 (PE detection), 0.65-0.75 (PE location), 0.48-0.51 (right heart strain), and 0.65-0.70 (image artifacts). Moderate temperature tuning (0.2-0.5) improved accuracy, while excessive in-context examples reduced performance. A dual-model review framework achieved >80-90% precision. LLMs demonstrate strong potential for automating PE registry abstraction, minimizing manual workload while preserving accuracy.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the feasibility and accuracy of using publicly available Large Language Models (LLMs), specifically LLaMA models, for automated clinical concept extraction from Computed Tomography Pulmonary Embolism (CTPE) reports. The goal is to streamline the process of populating PERT Consortium registries, which are currently reliant on manual abstraction. The study evaluates different LLaMA model sizes, versions, temperature settings, and in-context examples. The authors compared the LLM-extracted data to a human-curated gold standard using Cohen's kappa coefficient to assess agreement. The research also introduces a dual-model validation framework, using Phi-4 14B as an independent reviewer to enhance quality control. The results indicate that larger models (70B) perform better than smaller models (8B), moderate temperature tuning improves accuracy, and overly extensive in-context examples can hinder performance. The dual-model review shows promising precision.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies primarily in its application of recent LLaMA models to the specific problem of PE registry abstraction. Prior work has explored LLMs in medical text processing generally, and in radiology report analysis, but this study focuses on a well-defined, high-stakes clinical condition (PE) and uses the findings to propose a concrete workflow (including a dual-model approach) for a key clinical application. Exploring the effects of model size, temperature tuning, and in-context learning examples on the *performance specific for the medical concept extraction task* contributes incrementally to the field. The dual-model approach for quality control is a valuable contribution.

*   **Significance:** The significance of the work stems from the potential to automate and scale the population of clinical registries. Manual abstraction is a bottleneck, limiting the utility of these registries for research and quality improvement. If LLMs can reliably extract clinical data, this could lead to more efficient data collection, faster insights, and ultimately improved patient care. The study's findings provide practical guidance on how to effectively use LLMs in this context, including the importance of model size and careful temperature tuning. It provides insight into the optimal approach for this medical concept extraction task which can save valuable time and money in the healthcare space.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Systematic evaluation of different LLM configurations (size, versions, temperature, examples).
    *   Comparison to a human-curated gold standard using appropriate statistical measures (Cohen's kappa).
    *   The inclusion of data from two different sources (MIMIC-IV and DUHS EHR) enhances external validity.
    *   Introduction of a dual-model review framework for quality control.
    *   The clearly defined use case and potential impact on clinical practice.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The paper focuses on a limited set of concepts related to PE. Generalizability to other medical domains or more complex clinical concepts needs further investigation.
    *   The study does not fully explore fine-tuning strategies (LoRA, etc.), which could potentially further improve LLM performance.
    *   The computational cost of using larger LLMs (70B) can be prohibitive for some institutions.  The authors acknowledge this limitation, but a more detailed discussion of cost-benefit trade-offs would be helpful.
    *   Although the authors performed a calibration exercise, they did not discuss how frequently the consensus process was required.

*   **Influence on the Field:** The paper has the potential to influence the field by demonstrating the feasibility of using LLMs for clinical registry abstraction and by providing practical recommendations for implementation. The dual-model validation framework is a promising approach that could be adopted by other researchers and practitioners. Follow-up studies could focus on prospective validation, incorporating a broader range of clinical concepts, and exploring fine-tuning strategies.

**Score: 7**

**Rationale:** The paper makes a valuable contribution to the field by demonstrating the potential of LLMs for automating clinical registry abstraction. Its systematic evaluation of different LLM configurations and the introduction of a dual-model validation framework are key strengths. However, the limitations related to the scope of clinical concepts, lack of fine-tuning exploration, and computational cost prevent it from being a truly groundbreaking work. While it advances the application of LLMs in healthcare, it builds upon existing research and leaves room for further investigation. The novelty lies in the *specific application* and methodical approach to *parameter tuning* for this medical domain.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Can Large Language Models Predict Associations Among Human Attitudes?
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.21011v1)
- **Authors**: Ana Ma, Derek Powell
- **Abstract**: Prior work has shown that large language models (LLMs) can predict human attitudes based on other attitudes, but this work has largely focused on predictions from highly similar and interrelated attitudes. In contrast, human attitudes are often strongly associated even across disparate and dissimilar topics. Using a novel dataset of human responses toward diverse attitude statements, we found that a frontier language model (GPT-4o) was able to recreate the pairwise correlations among individual attitudes and to predict individuals' attitudes from one another. Crucially, in an advance over prior work, we tested GPT-4o's ability to predict in the absence of surface-similarity between attitudes, finding that while surface similarity improves prediction accuracy, the model was still highly-capable of generating meaningful social inferences between dissimilar attitudes. Altogether, our findings indicate that LLMs capture crucial aspects of the deeper, latent structure of human belief systems.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

This paper investigates the ability of large language models (LLMs), specifically GPT-4o, to predict human attitudes based on other attitudes, even when the topics are dissimilar and lack obvious semantic overlap. The authors created a dataset of US adults' responses to a wide range of opinion questions from Pew surveys. They then tested GPT-4o's ability to recreate pairwise correlations among individual attitudes and to predict attitudes from one another, assessing the influence of semantic similarity. The results indicate that GPT-4o can meaningfully recreate correlational patterns and predict attitudes beyond simple surface-level similarity, suggesting a capability for deeper social inference and capturing the latent structure of human belief systems. They found that while semantic similarity biases the predictions and leads to better accuracy with similar items, GPT-4o is still able to make reasonable inferences even when attitudes are dissimilar. The authors acknowledge the potential risks of LLMs being used for manipulation and discuss limitations of their study.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty of the paper lies in examining LLMs' ability to infer relationships between *dissimilar* attitudes, a crucial aspect of human social reasoning. Previous work has focused primarily on predicting attitudes from similar and related ones. This emphasis on disparate attitudes is a clear advance.
*   **Significance:** This work is significant for several reasons:
    *   It demonstrates that LLMs are not simply pattern-matching based on semantic overlap but can capture aspects of the deeper, potentially ideological, structure of human beliefs.
    *   It contributes to the ongoing debate about LLMs' capacity for genuine understanding versus superficial processing.
    *   It raises important ethical concerns about the potential for LLMs to be used for persuasion and manipulation.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Well-defined research question:** The paper clearly addresses the gap in understanding LLMs' abilities regarding attitude prediction across disparate topics.
    *   **Rigorous Methodology:** The authors use a combination of human data collection, LLM prompting, and statistical analysis. They test the impact of semantic similarity in various ways to isolate LLMs' abilities beyond pattern matching.
    *   **Clearly explained results:** The results are clearly presented and interpreted. The figures help visualize the findings and the discussion of the effects of semantic similarity on prediction accuracy is nuanced.
    *   **Ethical awareness:** The paper acknowledges and discusses the potential ethical implications of its findings, demonstrating a sense of responsibility.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited exploration of different prompting strategies:** While the prompt engineering and temperature are discussed, there may be more complex prompting approaches that would further increase the LLMs performance, such as more sophisticated chain-of-thought approaches, or including prior information that allows the LLM to explicitly generate the target ideology being assumed. The authors acknowledged the limits in their sampling and prompting approach.
    *   **Reliance on a single LLM (GPT-4o):** While GPT-4o is a powerful model, testing other LLMs would provide a broader understanding of the generalization of these findings and would help in determining whether the results are specific to certain LLM architectures or pre-training datasets.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper makes a substantial contribution by demonstrating that LLMs can infer relationships between dissimilar attitudes, which is a more challenging and ecologically valid task than simply predicting attitudes from similar ones. While the paper could have been strengthened by more extensive prompting and the use of other LLMs, the research question is clearly defined, the methodology is sound, the results are clearly presented, and the discussion demonstrates a good understanding of the broader implications of the research. Considering the novelty and significance of the study in the field of LLM and social cognition, I assign the paper a score of:

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Scalability Evaluation of HPC Multi-GPU Training for ECG-based LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.21033v1)
- **Authors**: Dimitar Mileski, Nikola Petrovski, Marjan Gusev
- **Abstract**: Training large language models requires extensive processing, made possible by many high-performance computing resources. This study compares multi-node and multi-GPU environments for training large language models of electrocardiograms. It provides a detailed mapping of current frameworks for distributed deep learning in multinode and multi-GPU settings, including Horovod from Uber, DeepSpeed from Microsoft, and the built-in distributed capabilities of PyTorch and TensorFlow. We compare various multi-GPU setups for different dataset configurations, utilizing multiple HPC nodes independently and focusing on scalability, speedup, efficiency, and overhead. The analysis leverages HPC infrastructure with SLURM, Apptainer (Singularity) containers, CUDA, PyTorch, and shell scripts to support training workflows and automation. We achieved a sub-linear speedup when scaling the number of GPUs, with values of 1.6x for two and 1.9x for four.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the scalability of HPC (High-Performance Computing) resources for training large language models (LLMs) specifically tailored for electrocardiogram (ECG) data. It compares multi-node and multi-GPU configurations using various distributed deep learning frameworks like Horovod, DeepSpeed, and native PyTorch/TensorFlow. The study evaluates the performance (speedup, efficiency, overhead) of training an ECG-based LLM on the MeluXina supercomputer.  The key findings highlight sub-linear speedups when scaling the number of GPUs and identify an optimal sliding window size for processing ECG data. The paper aims to optimize the training process for these computationally intensive models, acknowledging the constraints of HPC resource allocation.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a practical study of applying distributed training to ECG-based LLMs on a specific HPC platform (MeluXina). While the use of LLMs for ECG analysis and distributed training techniques are not individually novel, their combination and practical evaluation in a constrained HPC environment have some merit. The ECG-specific focus is also somewhat unique. The study also examines various CPU and GPU configurations. The comparison of frameworks such as Horovod, DeepSpeed and PyTorch in the context of ECGs is also not often found.

*   **Significance:** The paper offers practical insights for researchers and practitioners working with ECG data and LLMs in HPC settings. Identifying the optimal configurations and limitations of GPU scaling contributes to more efficient use of HPC resources. The results could help optimize training workflows and reduce the overall computational cost associated with building such models. The investigation into sliding window size for processing ECG data is helpful and adds value.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Practical Focus:** The paper addresses a real-world problem of training computationally intensive models in a resource-constrained environment.
    *   **Detailed Evaluation:** The evaluation methodology is clearly defined, and the metrics (speedup, efficiency, overhead) are appropriate.
    *   **Framework Comparison:** Provides a comparative analysis of different distributed training frameworks.
    *   **Specific Focus:** The ECG-specific focus adds a layer of specialization that is valuable for researchers in that field.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Novelty:** The core techniques (distributed training, LLMs) are well-established. The novelty primarily lies in the specific application to ECG data and the particular HPC environment.
    *   **Sub-linear Scaling:** The reported sub-linear speedups are not unexpected but still represent a limitation. A more in-depth analysis of the causes for less-than-ideal scaling (e.g., communication bottlenecks) would be beneficial.
    *   **MeluXina Specificity:** The findings are somewhat specific to the MeluXina supercomputer. While this provides concrete results, it limits the generalizability of the conclusions. The hardware and software architecture can influence performance.
    *   **Lack of code optimisation details**: The research focuses on the general findings of the framework with GPU configuration but does not explicitly talk about changes or code optimizations made within the framework

*   **Potential Impact:** The study has the potential to influence researchers working on similar problems by providing a reference point for optimal configurations and highlighting the limitations of GPU scaling. The insights into sliding window sizes are valuable. The paper could inform the development of more efficient training strategies and resource allocation policies.

*   **Justification of Score:** Given the limitations regarding the novelty of the techniques and the focus on a specific environment, a score of 6 is assigned. While the practical evaluation and specific findings for ECG-based LLMs in an HPC setting are valuable, the core concepts are not groundbreaking. The paper offers a solid applied study that can contribute to the field, but more in-depth analysis of bottlenecks and broader generalizability would increase its impact.

### What Changed and What Could Have Changed? State-Change Counterfactuals for Procedure-Aware Video Representation Learning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.21055v1)
- **Authors**: Chi-Hsi Kung, Frangil Ramirez, Juhyung Ha, Yi-Ting Chen, David Crandall, Yi-Hsuan Tsai
- **Abstract**: Understanding a procedural activity requires modeling both how action steps transform the scene, and how evolving scene transformations can influence the sequence of action steps, even those that are accidental or erroneous. Existing work has studied procedure-aware video representations by proposing novel approaches such as modeling the temporal order of actions and has not explicitly learned the state changes (scene transformations). In this work, we study procedure-aware video representation learning by incorporating state-change descriptions generated by Large Language Models (LLMs) as supervision signals for video encoders. Moreover, we generate state-change counterfactuals that simulate hypothesized failure outcomes, allowing models to learn by imagining the unseen ``What if'' scenarios. This counterfactual reasoning facilitates the model's ability to understand the cause and effect of each step in an activity. To verify the procedure awareness of our model, we conduct extensive experiments on procedure-aware tasks, including temporal action segmentation and error detection. Our results demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed state-change descriptions and their counterfactuals and achieve significant improvements on multiple tasks. We will make our source code and data publicly available soon.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a hierarchical procedure-aware video representation learning framework that incorporates state-change descriptions and their counterfactuals, generated using a Large Language Model (LLM). At the clip level, the model captures "before" and "after" states to identify action-induced state changes, along with state-change counterfactuals simulating hypothetical failure scenarios. At the video level, the framework employs missing-step and misordered counterfactuals to enhance understanding of the entire procedure. The model is pre-trained on Ego4D and evaluated on temporal action segmentation and error detection tasks, demonstrating improved performance compared to baselines.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The key novelty lies in the explicit incorporation of state-change descriptions and *counterfactual* reasoning for procedure-aware video representation learning.  Previous work often focused on temporal ordering of actions or action labels as supervision but did not explicitly model the state changes induced by actions or, crucially, hypothesize about alternative (failure) outcomes.  The use of LLMs to generate these descriptions is a practical approach, but not entirely novel in itself, as LLMs are increasingly used for data augmentation and annotation.  The hierarchical approach, considering both clip-level and video-level counterfactuals, is a further contribution that builds upon the base concept.

* **Significance:** The ability to model state changes and reason about alternative outcomes (counterfactuals) significantly enhances the model's understanding of procedural activities.  This is important for downstream tasks like error detection and temporal action segmentation, where understanding cause-and-effect relationships is crucial. The experimental results show improved performance on these tasks, indicating the effectiveness of the proposed approach. The potential impact lies in developing more robust and reliable systems for applications such as robot learning, video retrieval, and intelligent collaborative agents.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Explicit state-change modeling:** Addresses a gap in existing procedure-aware video representation learning.
    * **Counterfactual reasoning:** Allows the model to learn from hypothesized failure scenarios, improving robustness.
    * **Hierarchical framework:** Captures information at both clip-level and video-level, providing a comprehensive understanding of the procedure.
    * **Strong experimental results:** Demonstrates the effectiveness of the approach on challenging downstream tasks.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Reliance on LLMs:**  The performance is heavily dependent on the quality of state-change descriptions and counterfactuals generated by the LLM.  Biases or inaccuracies in the LLM could negatively impact the model's learning. The paper acknowledges using LLama, but does not critically analyze potential limitations of LLMs in the context of generating accurate and relevant counterfactuals.
    * **Limited analysis of LLM quality:** The paper mentions refining the LLM's output, but does not extensively evaluate how the specific quality and diversity of LLM generations influence the performance. A more detailed study of this aspect would strengthen the findings.
    * **Incremental improvement:** While the results demonstrate improvement, the magnitude of the gains might be considered incremental compared to some baselines. While it outperforms HierVL, it only shows modest gains against the best performing models like Bridge-Prompt, depending on the metric.
    * **Limited scope of tasks:** The evaluation is focused on temporal action segmentation and error detection. Exploring other downstream tasks, especially those where understanding of *state* is most salient, may better illustrate the significance.

* **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research in procedure-aware video understanding by highlighting the importance of state-change modeling and counterfactual reasoning. It also presents a practical framework for incorporating these concepts into video representation learning using LLMs. However, the community needs to critically examine if the LLM based generation is the best avenue to develop state understanding and consider other potential pathways.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a novel and well-executed approach to procedure-aware video representation learning by explicitly modeling state changes and using counterfactual reasoning. The use of LLMs for generating state descriptions and counterfactuals is a practical approach.  The experimental results demonstrate improved performance on relevant downstream tasks. However, there are questions about the dependency on LLMs, incremental performance gain, and limited task domains.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Online Reasoning Video Segmentation with Just-in-Time Digital Twins
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.21056v1)
- **Authors**: Yiqing Shen, Bohan Liu, Chenjia Li, Lalithkumar Seenivasan, Mathias Unberath
- **Abstract**: Reasoning segmentation (RS) aims to identify and segment objects of interest based on implicit text queries. As such, RS is a catalyst for embodied AI agents, enabling them to interpret high-level commands without requiring explicit step-by-step guidance. However, current RS approaches rely heavily on the visual perception capabilities of multimodal large language models (LLMs), leading to several major limitations. First, they struggle with queries that require multiple steps of reasoning or those that involve complex spatial/temporal relationships. Second, they necessitate LLM fine-tuning, which may require frequent updates to maintain compatibility with contemporary LLMs and may increase risks of catastrophic forgetting during fine-tuning. Finally, being primarily designed for static images or offline video processing, they scale poorly to online video data. To address these limitations, we propose an agent framework that disentangles perception and reasoning for online video RS without LLM fine-tuning. Our innovation is the introduction of a just-in-time digital twin concept, where -- given an implicit query -- a LLM plans the construction of a low-level scene representation from high-level video using specialist vision models. We refer to this approach to creating a digital twin as "just-in-time" because the LLM planner will anticipate the need for specific information and only request this limited subset instead of always evaluating every specialist model. The LLM then performs reasoning on this digital twin representation to identify target objects. To evaluate our approach, we introduce a new comprehensive video reasoning segmentation benchmark comprising 200 videos with 895 implicit text queries. The benchmark spans three reasoning categories (semantic, spatial, and temporal) with three different reasoning chain complexity.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel framework for online video reasoning segmentation (RS).  It addresses limitations of current RS approaches that heavily rely on multimodal LLMs, particularly their struggle with complex reasoning chains, the need for frequent LLM fine-tuning, and poor scalability to online video data.  The proposed solution is an agent-based architecture that disentangles perception and reasoning. It introduces a "just-in-time" digital twin concept, where an LLM plans the construction of a low-level scene representation using specialist vision models based on the specific query. This allows the LLM to reason on a more efficient and detailed representation than directly processing the video. The framework is evaluated on a new comprehensive video RS benchmark and demonstrates improved performance compared to existing methods, especially in complex reasoning tasks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

The paper's novelty lies in several key aspects:

*   **Just-in-time Digital Twin:** The core idea of dynamically constructing a scene representation tailored to the specific query is a significant departure from methods that attempt to directly process video or rely on fixed-length token representations in LLMs. This adaptive approach optimizes both computational efficiency and reasoning accuracy.

*   **Disentanglement of Perception and Reasoning:**  Separating the perception and reasoning stages enables the use of specialized vision models for detailed scene understanding while reserving the LLM for high-level reasoning. This alleviates the burden on the LLM to perform low-level perception and avoids the limitations of compressing visual information into LLM tokens.

*   **LLM-Coder for Spatial-Temporal Reasoning:**  The introduction of an LLM-coder to translate abstract reasoning into concrete operations on the digital twin is a clever way to handle spatial and temporal relationships without needing extensive LLM fine-tuning for these specific skills.

*   **New Benchmark:** The introduction of a new video reasoning segmentation benchmark is a valuable contribution, providing a standardized platform for evaluating future RS methods, especially concerning multi-step and spatial/temporal reasoning.

**Significance:**

The paper addresses critical limitations in current RS research, making it significant for the following reasons:

*   **Improved Performance:** The experimental results clearly demonstrate that the proposed framework outperforms existing methods on the new benchmark, especially in tasks requiring complex reasoning and spatial/temporal understanding.

*   **Increased Efficiency:** By constructing a query-specific digital twin, the framework reduces computational overhead compared to methods that process entire video sequences through LLMs.

*   **Enhanced Maintainability:**  Eliminating the need for LLM fine-tuning reduces the risk of catastrophic forgetting and simplifies maintenance as LLMs evolve. The modularity of the approach, using the LLM and specialist vision models, makes it easily updatable to use better or newer models.

*   **Broader Applicability:** The framework's online processing capabilities and improved reasoning abilities make it more suitable for real-world embodied AI applications than existing offline approaches.

**Weaknesses:**

Despite the strengths, some weaknesses should be acknowledged:

*   **Dependency on LLMs:** While the framework avoids LLM fine-tuning, it still depends on the performance and capabilities of the LLMs (base LLM and LLM-coder). This inherent dependency can potentially constrain its performance.

*   **Complexity:** The framework is more complex than existing approaches, involving multiple components (LLM planner, specialist vision models, LLM-coder, digital twin construction, etc.). This complexity may increase development and deployment costs.

*   **Limited Experiments:**  The ablation study provides insights into the contribution of each component, but further experiments could explore the impact of different vision models, LLM architectures, and prompting strategies.

**Overall Score and Justification:**

I assign a score of **8/10**. The paper presents a significant contribution to the field of reasoning segmentation by addressing several crucial limitations of current approaches. The "just-in-time" digital twin concept is a novel and effective way to bridge the gap between high-level reasoning and low-level visual perception. The experimental results are convincing, and the introduction of a new benchmark is a valuable asset to the community. The primary reason for not assigning a higher score is the inherent dependency on LLMs, the increased complexity of the framework, and potential computational costs in using different specialist vision models. However, the potential benefits and impact of this framework on embodied AI are substantial.

**In summary, the paper presents a well-designed and thoroughly evaluated framework that advances the state-of-the-art in online video reasoning segmentation. Its modular approach, improved performance, and online processing capabilities make it a significant contribution to the field.**

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 0/10

### Efficient Multi-Instance Generation with Janus-Pro-Dirven Prompt Parsing
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.21069v1)
- **Authors**: Fan Qi, Yu Duan, Changsheng Xu
- **Abstract**: Recent advances in text-guided diffusion models have revolutionized conditional image generation, yet they struggle to synthesize complex scenes with multiple objects due to imprecise spatial grounding and limited scalability. We address these challenges through two key modules: 1) Janus-Pro-driven Prompt Parsing, a prompt-layout parsing module that bridges text understanding and layout generation via a compact 1B-parameter architecture, and 2) MIGLoRA, a parameter-efficient plug-in integrating Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) into UNet (SD1.5) and DiT (SD3) backbones. MIGLoRA is capable of preserving the base model's parameters and ensuring plug-and-play adaptability, minimizing architectural intrusion while enabling efficient fine-tuning. To support a comprehensive evaluation, we create DescripBox and DescripBox-1024, benchmarks that span diverse scenes and resolutions. The proposed method achieves state-of-the-art performance on COCO and LVIS benchmarks while maintaining parameter efficiency, demonstrating superior layout fidelity and scalability for open-world synthesis.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel approach to multi-instance generation (MIG) using text-guided diffusion models. It tackles challenges related to spatial grounding and scalability in complex scenes. The core contributions are: 1) Janus-Pro-driven Prompt Parsing, a module that bridges text understanding and layout generation using a compact language model; and 2) MIGLoRA, a parameter-efficient plug-in that integrates Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) into diffusion backbones (SD1.5 and SD3). MIGLoRA preserves base model parameters and enables efficient fine-tuning. The authors also create two new benchmarks, DescripBox and DescripBox-1024, to evaluate the method across various scenes and resolutions. The proposed method achieves state-of-the-art performance on COCO and LVIS while maintaining parameter efficiency.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper introduces several novel components, but their individual novelty is somewhat incremental.  The use of Janus-Pro for layout parsing is interesting, leveraging its combined understanding and generation capabilities, but Janus-Pro itself is pre-existing. MIGLoRA's LoRA integration is a strong point for parameter efficiency, but LoRA is also an established technique. The clever aspect is in adapting it *specifically* for multi-instance generation, particularly the mask-driven feature concatenation and RoPE-inspired encoding, which enhances spatial coherence. The curated datasets, DescripBox and DescripBox-1024, address a genuine need for comprehensive MIG evaluation and are a valuable contribution to the field.
* **Significance:**  The paper offers a significant contribution to multi-instance generation by addressing both spatial grounding and scalability issues. The parameter-efficient nature of MIGLoRA makes it practical for real-world applications and open-world synthesis. The state-of-the-art results on standard benchmarks and the introduction of the new, challenging datasets clearly demonstrate the effectiveness of the approach. The integration of Janus-Pro for prompt parsing is a useful contribution, providing better layout adherence. The improvement in zero-shot spatial localization on LVIS further solidifies the significance of the work. A key advantage over related works (e.g., HiCo, MtDM) is the reduction in computational cost, specifically parameter count, without a severe drop in performance.
* **Strengths:**
    * **Parameter Efficiency:** MIGLoRA is a major strength, enabling strong performance with minimal fine-tuning.
    * **Comprehensive Evaluation:**  The paper provides thorough quantitative results on multiple datasets (COCO, LVIS, DescripBox), covering both standard and zero-shot scenarios.  The ablation studies are well-designed and provide insight into the importance of each component.
    * **New Datasets:** DescripBox and DescripBox-1024 fill a gap in the evaluation of multi-instance generation.
    * **Strong Performance:** State-of-the-art or competitive performance on benchmark datasets.
* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Incremental Novelty:** While the combination of techniques is novel, the individual components (Janus-Pro, LoRA) are not entirely new.
    * **Limited Qualitative Analysis:** While the paper includes some qualitative results, a more in-depth analysis of the model's failure cases and limitations would be beneficial.  For example, discussing scenarios where the model struggles with complex relationships between objects or in extremely crowded scenes.
    * **Dataset focus:** While novel and helpful, the DescripBox dataset's focus appears to be driven more from the capabilities of Grounded-SAM rather than a specific set of needs in the research area.

* **Potential Influence:** The paper is likely to influence future research in multi-instance generation by demonstrating the effectiveness of parameter-efficient fine-tuning and the importance of layout parsing. The new datasets will serve as a valuable resource for evaluating MIG models. The clear and well-documented methodology will facilitate adoption by other researchers.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper demonstrates significant improvements in multi-instance generation, particularly in parameter efficiency and scalability. The introduction of new datasets and strong benchmark results are compelling. While the novelty of the individual components is somewhat incremental, the overall design and integration of these components is well-executed and leads to state-of-the-art performance. The strengths of the paper outweigh its weaknesses, making it a valuable contribution to the field. Therefore, a score of 7 is appropriate.
Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CV

### Can Video Diffusion Model Reconstruct 4D Geometry?
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.21082v1)
- **Authors**: Jinjie Mai, Wenxuan Zhu, Haozhe Liu, Bing Li, Cheng Zheng, Jürgen Schmidhuber, Bernard Ghanem
- **Abstract**: Reconstructing dynamic 3D scenes (i.e., 4D geometry) from monocular video is an important yet challenging problem. Conventional multiview geometry-based approaches often struggle with dynamic motion, whereas recent learning-based methods either require specialized 4D representation or sophisticated optimization. In this paper, we present Sora3R, a novel framework that taps into the rich spatiotemporal priors of large-scale video diffusion models to directly infer 4D pointmaps from casual videos. Sora3R follows a two-stage pipeline: (1) we adapt a pointmap VAE from a pretrained video VAE, ensuring compatibility between the geometry and video latent spaces; (2) we finetune a diffusion backbone in combined video and pointmap latent space to generate coherent 4D pointmaps for every frame. Sora3R operates in a fully feedforward manner, requiring no external modules (e.g., depth, optical flow, or segmentation) or iterative global alignment. Extensive experiments demonstrate that Sora3R reliably recovers both camera poses and detailed scene geometry, achieving performance on par with state-of-the-art methods for dynamic 4D reconstruction across diverse scenarios.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Sora3R, a novel framework for reconstructing dynamic 3D scenes (4D geometry) from monocular video. It leverages the spatiotemporal priors learned by large-scale video diffusion models (VDMs) to directly infer 4D pointmaps from casual videos. The method consists of two stages: 1) Adapting a pointmap Variational Autoencoder (VAE) from a pretrained video VAE to ensure compatibility between geometry and video latent spaces. 2) Fine-tuning a diffusion backbone in a combined video and pointmap latent space to generate coherent 4D pointmaps for each frame.  Sora3R operates in a feedforward manner, without needing external modules or iterative global alignment. Experiments demonstrate the recovery of camera poses and scene geometry, achieving performance comparable to state-of-the-art methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The primary novelty lies in the direct application of video diffusion models for 4D geometry reconstruction, specifically pointmaps.  While depth estimation using DMs exists (cited in the paper), the holistic 4D pointmap reconstruction from a *single* video using this approach represents a distinct contribution. Fine-tuning the pointmap VAE from a pre-trained video VAE is also a useful adaptation to bridge the domain gap.

*   **Significance:** The significance stems from potentially simplifying dynamic 3D scene understanding. Existing methods often require specialized 4D representations or complex optimization.  Sora3R's feedforward nature and reliance on pre-trained VDMs offer a more efficient alternative.  The ability to infer camera pose and scene geometry without external modules like optical flow or segmentation is also a notable advantage. This potentially lowers the barriers to entry for dynamic scene reconstruction and opens avenues for downstream 3D/4D tasks. However, the current performance falls short of the strongest existing methods, somewhat diminishing the immediate impact. This indicates that leveraging all of the learned information embedded within large-scale DMs is still an open problem.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Direct and relatively simple pipeline compared to many existing methods.
    *   Leverages the power of large-scale pre-trained video diffusion models, reducing the need for extensive annotated 4D datasets.
    *   Feedforward architecture enables efficient inference.
    *   Provides both camera pose and dense scene reconstruction.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   Performance, while comparable to state-of-the-art, isn't consistently superior, especially on static scenes.
    *   Still struggles with camera pose estimation in challenging scenarios as detailed in the limitations.
    *   Relies on noisy depth and poses during pretraining (although mostly mitigated by synthetic dataset finetuning).
    *   Potential reliance on computational resources required to train or finetune DMs, which hinders accessibility.
    *   Inference still requires VAE encoding of the video which will require substantial memory.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper could influence future research by:
    *   Encouraging the community to explore generative model-based approaches for 3D reconstruction.
    *   Demonstrating the feasibility of leveraging pre-trained VDMs for geometric tasks.
    *   Inspiring new architectures that better integrate the strengths of VDMs with geometric constraints.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a novel approach that offers potential advantages in terms of simplicity and efficiency, however, the paper has a few significant weaknesses that need to be solved before it can be groundbreaking. While Sora3R demonstrates promising results, the current performance gap compared to existing methods, especially in static scenes, limits its immediate practical impact. The reliance on powerful diffusion models, while a strength in many ways, could also be a barrier to adoption for some researchers and practitioners. Furthermore, it's not entirely clear how the method will scale to larger, more complex scenes without further architectural improvements.

### ZJUKLAB at SemEval-2025 Task 4: Unlearning via Model Merging
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.21088v1)
- **Authors**: Haoming Xu, Shuxun Wang, Yanqiu Zhao, Yi Zhong, Ziyan Jiang, Ningyuan Zhao, Shumin Deng, Huajun Chen, Ningyu Zhang
- **Abstract**: This paper presents the ZJUKLAB team's submission for SemEval-2025 Task 4: Unlearning Sensitive Content from Large Language Models. This task aims to selectively erase sensitive knowledge from large language models, avoiding both over-forgetting and under-forgetting issues. We propose an unlearning system that leverages Model Merging (specifically TIES-Merging), combining two specialized models into a more balanced unlearned model. Our system achieves competitive results, ranking second among 26 teams, with an online score of 0.944 for Task Aggregate and 0.487 for overall Aggregate. In this paper, we also conduct local experiments and perform a comprehensive analysis of the unlearning process, examining performance trajectories, loss dynamics, and weight perspectives, along with several supplementary experiments, to understand the effectiveness of our method. Furthermore, we analyze the shortcomings of our method and evaluation metrics, emphasizing that MIA scores and ROUGE-based metrics alone are insufficient to fully evaluate successful unlearning. Finally, we emphasize the need for more comprehensive evaluation methodologies and rethinking of unlearning objectives in future research. Code is available at https://github.com/zjunlp/unlearn/tree/main/semeval25.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the ZJUKLAB SemEval-2025 Task 4 paper:

**Summary:**

The paper describes ZJUKLAB's approach to the SemEval-2025 Task 4: Unlearning Sensitive Content from Large Language Models. They propose a model merging technique (TIES-Merging) where two specialized models – one that over-forgets and one that under-forgets – are combined to achieve a more balanced unlearned model. They achieve second place in the competition. The paper provides an analysis of the unlearning process, including performance trajectories, loss dynamics, and weight perspectives.  It highlights shortcomings in current evaluation metrics like MIA scores and ROUGE-based measures. The authors suggest a need for more comprehensive unlearning evaluation and a re-evaluation of the objectives.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Strengths:**

    * **Model Merging for Unlearning:** The core idea of model merging to balance over-forgetting and under-forgetting is novel. It provides a practical approach to tackle a key challenge in unlearning. Combining models with complementary biases makes intuitive sense.
    * **Comprehensive Analysis:** The paper goes beyond simply reporting the results. It includes in-depth analysis of performance trajectories, loss dynamics, and weight perspectives. The ablation studies on density in TIES-Merging offer valuable insights.
    * **Highlighting Limitations of Evaluation:** The authors' honest discussion of the limitations of existing evaluation metrics (MIA and ROUGE-based) is important. They correctly point out that current metrics can be easily gamed or do not fully capture the complexities of unlearning.
    * **Practical Relevance:** Unlearning is becoming increasingly important for AI safety and compliance. This work contributes to a practical approach to this challenge.

* **Weaknesses:**

    * **Reliance on LoRA and TIES-Merging:** While LoRA and TIES-Merging are effective techniques, they're not inherently novel. The novelty lies in their *application* to the unlearning problem. The paper might overemphasize the novelty of these techniques themselves.
    * **Limited Generalizability:**  The specific hyperparameters and training procedures are tailored to the SemEval task. It's not clear how well this specific setup would generalize to other datasets or unlearning scenarios.  The authors acknowledge domain shifts as potential issue affecting MIA scores.
    * **Online MIA Score Discrepancies:**  The difference between the local and online MIA scores raises questions about the reliability of the online evaluation data.  A more robust analysis of this discrepancy would strengthen the paper.
    * **Repetitive Outputs:** A significant weakness is the generation of repetitive output, which indicates a failure to fully unlearn/generalize correctly.
    * **Limited Theoretical Justification:** While the empirical results are promising, the paper lacks a deep theoretical justification for why TIES-Merging works so well in this context. A theoretical understanding would allow for better optimization and wider applicability.

* **Novelty and Significance:**

The paper introduces a pragmatic approach to unlearning using model merging. The combination of over-forgetting and under-forgetting model through TIES-Merging presents an interesting method of balancing privacy and performance. The paper contributes to the understanding of current limitations for evaluation and objectives, which can inspire future research.

* **Potential Influence:**

The paper could influence future work by:

    * Encouraging more research into model merging for unlearning.
    * Motivating the development of more robust and comprehensive evaluation metrics for unlearning.
    * Pushing the community to rethink the objectives of unlearning, moving beyond simply suppressing original tokens.

* **Overall Assessment:**

The paper demonstrates a practical solution to a difficult problem. While the techniques used are not groundbreaking in themselves, their application to the unlearning problem, along with the comprehensive analysis and critique of existing methods and metrics, provides value to the research community. The method's limitations are openly discussed, promoting a more honest perspective on the challenges of unlearning.
